\title{A Textbook about the Paper: "Parameter-Efficient Orthogonal Finetuning via Factorization"}

# Chapter 1: Foundations of Efficient Model Adaptation

## The Scalability Challenge: Training vs. Adapting Foundation Models

The landscape of artificial intelligence has undergone a seismic shift with the advent of large foundation models, such as ChatGPT and Stable Diffusion. These systems exhibit remarkable generalization capabilities across a vast array of tasks, driven by architectures containing hundreds of billions of parameters. For instance, models like GPT-3 possess approximately 175 billion parameters, a scale that renders the traditional practice of training a model from scratch prohibitively expensive for most researchers and organizations. The computational resources required to pretrain such massive networks are immense, involving vast amounts of energy and specialized hardware infrastructure that are often inaccessible. Consequently, broad scientific progress in this field now hinges not on building new models from the ground up, but on the ability to efficiently adapt existing, powerful foundation models to specific downstream tasks without the need for full retraining.

This necessity has created a critical distinction between the phases of model development: the initial pretraining phase and the subsequent adaptation phase. While pretraining aims to capture broad semantic knowledge from massive datasets, adaptation focuses on specializing this knowledge for particular applications. The challenge lies in performing this specialization efficiently. If adaptation were as costly as pretraining, the utility of foundation models would be severely limited to only those with access to supercomputing clusters. Therefore, the primary objective for modern machine learning practitioners is to develop methodologies that allow for task-specific optimization while keeping the computational footprint minimal. This requirement has spurred intense research into techniques that can modify a model's behavior significantly while altering only a tiny fraction of its parameters or requiring negligible additional compute during inference.

The stakes are high because inefficient adaptation methods can negate the very advantages provided by foundation models. If adapting a model requires nearly as much compute as training it, or if it introduces significant latency during deployment, the economic and practical viability of these technologies diminishes. Furthermore, simply freezing all parameters and hoping for transfer learning success is rarely sufficient; some degree of modification is almost always necessary to align the model with new data distributions or specific objectives. Thus, the field has moved toward identifying strategies that strike a delicate balance: modifying enough of the model to solve the target task effectively while preserving enough of the original architecture and learned representations to maintain stability and generalization. This tension defines the core problem space that efficient fine-tuning seeks to resolve.

## Overview of PEFT Paradigms: Full Fine-tuning, Adapters, and Prompt Tuning

To address the scalability challenge described above, researchers have developed three primary paradigms for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT): model fine-tuning (often referred to as partial or selective fine-tuning), adapter tuning, and prompt tuning. Each approach offers a unique mechanism for injecting task-specific knowledge into a pretrained backbone while attempting to minimize resource consumption. Understanding these distinctions is essential for grasping where newer methods fit into the broader ecosystem of model adaptation techniques.

Model fine-tuning represents perhaps the simplest yet most direct approach among these paradigms. In this method, the underlying architecture of the pretrained model remains entirely unchanged; instead, a subset of the existing model parameters is selected for optimization during training on downstream tasks. Unlike full fine-tuning where every weight is updated, efficient variants might freeze most layers and only update specific ones, or they might employ techniques like bit-fit which freezes all weights except bias terms and layernorm scales. The distinguishing advantage of pure model fine-tuning is its lack of inference latency; since no new architectural components are added to serve requests at runtime, the speed remains identical to that of the original frozen model. However, selecting which parameters to unfreeze often relies on heuristics or trial-and-error rather than rigorous theoretical grounding regarding what aspects of the representation should remain invariant versus what should be flexible.

Adapter tuning takes a different route by introducing additional trainable parameters into the network without modifying the original pretrained weights at all. These adapters are typically small modules inserted between existing layers—such as residual connections—that act as bottlenecks capable of learning task-specific transformations while leaving the bulk of the pretrained knowledge untouched in place. While highly effective at isolating new learning from old knowledge through modular design, adapter tuning inevitably increases memory usage during inference due to these extra stored parameters and adds computational overhead because data must pass through these additional layers every time a prediction is made. This trade-off between modularity and efficiency makes adapters attractive for scenarios where strict separation of concerns is needed but less ideal when inference speed is paramount compared to pure parameter selection methods like selective fine-tuning or orthogonal approaches discussed later in this chapter.

Prompt tuning operates fundamentally differently by manipulating inputs rather than internal weights directly associated with feature extraction layers deep inside transformer architectures like BERT # Chapter 1: Foundations of Efficient Model Adaptation

## The Scalability Challenge: Training vs. Adapting Foundation Models

The contemporary landscape of artificial intelligence is dominated by large foundation models such as ChatGPT and Stable Diffusion, which have demonstrated unprecedented generalization abilities across diverse domains including natural language processing and image synthesis. These achievements are underpinned by architectures containing staggering numbers of parameters; for instance, GPT-3 comprises approximately 175 billion parameters ($175 \times 10^9$). While this scale drives performance breakthroughs, it simultaneously creates a formidable barrier: training such models from scratch has become prohibitively expensive in terms of computational resources and energy consumption. Consequently, broad progress in machine learning research can no longer rely solely on building new models from zero; instead, it critically depends on our ability to efficiently adapt existing foundation models to specific downstream tasks without necessitating full-scale pretraining again. This shift necessitates methodologies that allow researchers to leverage pre-existing knowledge encoded within these massive networks while minimizing additional costs associated with customization for niche applications or proprietary datasets where complete transparency may not be feasible nor desirable depending upon organizational policies regarding intellectual property rights protection measures implemented internally within corporate environments globally operating today amidst rapidly evolving technological landscapes characterized by increasing complexity demands placed upon software engineering teams responsible maintaining secure robust scalable systems serving millions users daily worldwide across multiple continents spanning various industries sectors ranging finance # Chapter 2: The Information Transmission Framework

The pursuit of parameter efficiency in fine-tuning large foundation models often forces a trade-off between the expressivity of the learned transformations and the computational resources required to store them. In the context of Orthogonal Fine-tuning (OFT), the challenge lies in constructing a dense orthogonal matrix that can effectively rotate the feature space of a pretrained model without exploding the number of trainable parameters. Traditional approaches, such as using a full dense orthogonal matrix, require $\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ parameters for a dimension $d$, which is prohibitive for modern architectures. Conversely, restricting the matrix to a block-diagonal structure reduces parameter count but imposes rigid inductive biases that limit expressiveness. To resolve this tension, we introduce a novel conceptual shift: viewing the construction of a dense orthogonal matrix not merely as an algebraic exercise in matrix multiplication, but as an information transmission problem on a graph. This framework provides an intuitive geometric lens through which we can systematically design sparse factorizations that yield dense outcomes, ultimately leading to the development of the Orthogonal Butterfly (BOFT) method.

By reframing matrix factorization through the lens of graph theory, we move away from abstract linear algebra operations toward a visualization of connectivity and flow. In this view, a matrix is no longer just a grid of numbers but a map of relationships where non-zero entries represent active channels for information transfer. This perspective allows us to decompose complex transformations into simpler, sequential steps, much like routing signals through stages of a communication network. The goal becomes clear: we seek to arrange these sparse intermediate steps such that they collectively allow information to travel from any starting point to any destination, thereby creating an effective dense transformation, while minimizing the total number of "active wires" or trainable parameters required to build this network. This chapter details how this information transmission framework operates, defines the criteria for optimal designs within it, and contrasts traditional sparsity patterns with more flexible alternatives enabled by this new viewpoint.

# Reframing Matrix Factorization: From Algebra to Graph Theory

At its core, standard Orthogonal Fine-tuning involves updating a pretrained weight matrix $\bm{W}^0$ by multiplying it with a learnable orthogonal matrix $\bm{R}$. Mathematically, if $\bm{W}^0 \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times n}$ represents the frozen weights of a linear layer and $\bm{x} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ is an input vector, the forward pass computes $\bm{z} = (\bm{R}\bm{W}^0)^\top \bm{x}$. The objective is to learn $\bm{R}$ such that it adapts the model to downstream tasks while preserving pairwise angles between neurons. The difficulty arises when attempting to make $\bm{R}$ parameter-efficient. A naive solution might suggest simply setting many entries in $\bm{R}$ to zero (sparsification), but arbitrary sparsity destroys orthogonality and breaks the angle-preserving property essential for stable fine-tuning. Alternatively, one might attempt to factorize $\bm{R}$ into a product of smaller matrices, say $\bm{R} = \bm{B}_m \bm{B}_{m-1} \cdots \bm{B}_1$, hoping that each factor $\bm{B}_i$ can be made sparse while their product remains dense and orthogonal.

From an algebraic standpoint, analyzing which combinations of sparse matrices yield a dense product is cumbersome and lacks immediate intuition regarding *why* certain patterns work better than others. The information transmission framework resolves this by mapping the algebraic operation onto topological structures. We treat each dimension index $j$ in the vector space as a "node" in a graph. Consequently, a $d \times d$ matrix represents connectivity between $d$ source nodes and $d$ target nodes. If an entry $M_{ij}$ in matrix $M$ is non-zero, it signifies a directed edge allowing information (or signal) to flow from node $j$ at one level to node $i$ at the next level. If $M_{ij}$ is zero, no direct connection exists between these specific nodes at that step. When we multiply matrices sequentially ($\bm{B}_m \cdots \bm{B}_1$), we are effectively stacking these graphs vertically in time or depth. The resulting composite transformation describes whether there exists *any* path from an initial node at level 0 to a final node at level $m+1$.

This reframing transforms the problem from finding coefficients for polynomial-like expressions into designing efficient communication networks. Instead of asking "what values should I assign here?", we ask "how should I wire these nodes together?". In this graph-theoretic interpretation, generating a dense orthogonal matrix equivalent to ensuring that every node in the first layer can reach every node in the final layer through some sequence of edges provided by the intermediate sparse matrices $\bm{B}_i$. This shift is profound because it decouples the complexity of individual connections from their collective outcome. It allows us to reason about global density (connectivity) as an emergent property of local sparsity patterns arranged across multiple layers. Just as telecommunications engineers design routers and switches to maximize bandwidth while minimizing cabling costs, we can now design sparse orthogonal factors that maximize expressivity (global connectivity) while minimizing trainable parameters (total edges).

# Visualizing Matrix Multiplication as Information Flow on Grid Graphs

To fully grasp how sparse factors combine into dense products under this framework, one must visualize matrix multiplication as signal propagation through a grid-structured graph. Consider the factorization $\bm{R} = \bm{B}_m \bm{B}_{m-1} \cdots \bm{B}_1$. We can imagine this process occurring on a grid consisting of $d$ rows (representing dimensions) and $m+1$ columns (representing layers or time steps). At each column $k$, there are $d$ nodes corresponding to the dimensions at that stage of computation. The matrix $\bm{B}_k$ dictates how information moves from column $k$ to column $k+1$. Specifically, if $(\bm{B}_k)_{uv} \neq 0$, there is a directed edge connecting node $v$ at column $k$ to node $u$ at column $k+1$. The value zero implies no edge exists; thus, no direct transmission occurs between those specific nodes at that step.

When we compute the product $\bm{R}$, we are essentially tracing paths through this multi-layered grid. An entry $(\bm{R})_{xy}$ is non-zero if and only if there exists at least one valid path from node $x$ in column 0 (input) to node $y$ in column $m+1$ (output). If even one path exists involving non-zero edges across all intermediate matrices $\bm{B}_1$ through $\bm{B}_m$, then information originating at input dimension $x$ contributes to output dimension $y$, rendering $(\BM R)_{xy}$ potentially non-zero after optimization. Conversely, if no path connects them—meaning every possible chain of connections contains at least one broken link (a zero entry)—then $(\BM R)_{xy}$ will remain structurally zero regardless of parameter updates during training. This structural zero pattern determines whether our final transformation is truly capable of mixing all features globally or if it remains partitioned into isolated subsystems.

Consider a concrete example where we aim for full density with four dimensions ($d=4$). Suppose we use five sparse matrices ($\BM B_5 \dots \BM B_1$). If we examine partial products like $\BM B_4 \BM B_3 \BM B_2 \BM B_1$, we might find that information from input node 1 cannot reach output node 3 because all potential routes are blocked by zeros in earlier stages; consequently, position $(3,1)$ would be structurally zero in this partial product but could become non-zero once multiplied by subsequent matrices like $\BM B_5$. By unrolling these multiplications into layers on our grid graph, we can visually inspect exactly which pairs are connected and which are not before any numerical training begins. This visualization reveals that achieving global density does not require every single intermediate matrix $\BM B_k$ to be dense; rather, it requires their combined topology to form complete bipartite connectivity between start and finish nodes over time steps 0 and $m+1$. This insight liberates us from needing massive parameter counts per layer; instead, we only need sufficient diversity in connection patterns across layers so that every source eventually reaches every sink via some trajectory through the network depth-wise structure formed by successive applications thereof until reaching desired state space coverage entirely without redundancy loss due solely lack # Chapter 3: Butterfly Structures and Fast Linear Transforms

## Historical Context: The Cooley-Tukey FFT Algorithm

The quest for computational efficiency in handling large-scale data transformations dates back decades, culminating in one of the most influential algorithms in modern science: the Cooley-Tukey Fast Fourier Transform (FFT). Before this breakthrough, computing the Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT) required a direct matrix-vector multiplication involving a dense matrix of size $N \times N$, resulting in a computational complexity of $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$. For large datasets, this quadratic growth rendered real-time signal processing and spectral analysis practically impossible. In 1965, James Cooley and John Tukey revolutionized this landscape by introducing a divide-and-conquer strategy that decomposed an $N$-point DFT into two $\frac{N}{2}$-point DFTs. This recursive decomposition did not merely offer a slight optimization; it fundamentally altered the scaling behavior of the operation, reducing the complexity to $\mathcal{O}(N \log N)$.

The significance of the Cooley-Tukey algorithm extends far beyond mere speed; it revealed a profound structural insight regarding how information can be mixed efficiently across dimensions. The algorithm operates by breaking down a complex transformation into a sequence of simpler, localized operations. Instead of allowing every input element to interact with every output element simultaneously—a process requiring massive memory and compute resources—the FFT orchestrates these interactions through a series of stages. At each stage, pairs of data points are combined and separated according to specific rules, gradually building up the global frequency representation from local spatial correlations. This historical development established the "butterfly" as a canonical motif in numerical linear algebra, representing a mechanism where local changes propagate globally with minimal resource expenditure.

For the context of modern machine learning and parameter-efficient fine-tuning, the Cooley-Tukey algorithm serves as more than just a tool for signal processing; it acts as a conceptual blueprint for designing efficient neural network architectures. Just as the FFT allows for the rapid transformation of signals without storing or updating millions of redundant parameters, it suggests that complex linear transformations in deep learning can be achieved through structured compositions of sparse matrices. The algorithm demonstrates that high-dimensional mixing does not necessarily require dense connectivity at every step. By studying how the Cooley-Tukey method achieves global reach through local steps, we gain the intuition needed to construct orthogonal matrices that are both dense in their effect and sparse in their parameter count, bridging the gap between classical signal processing theory and contemporary adaptive modeling techniques.

## Anatomy of the Butterfly Graph Topology

To truly grasp how the butterfly structure facilitates efficient information transmission, one must visualize its underlying graph topology. In the context of matrix factorization, we can interpret a dense matrix multiplication as an information flow problem on a grid-structured graph with $d$ nodes per level and $m+1$ levels corresponding to $m$ sequential matrix multiplications. A standard dense orthogonal matrix would require every node at level $i$ to have a directed edge connecting to every node at level $i+1$, resulting in $\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ edges per layer and prohibitive parameter counts. The butterfly graph offers an elegant alternative by enforcing a strict sparsity pattern while maintaining full connectivity across layers.

The topology is constructed recursively. Starting with $d$ source nodes at level 0 and aiming for $d$ destination nodes at level $m$, where $m = \log_2 d$, the graph consists of intermediate layers where information is progressively mixed. At each stage, nodes are paired up based on binary representations of their indices. Specifically, two nodes connected by an edge differ only in one bit position determined by the current recursion depth. This creates a distinctive "X" shape or "butterfly" pattern between adjacent levels: two inputs enter from below, cross paths or remain parallel depending on their phase relationship, and emerge as outputs above. Crucially, this structure ensures that information originating from any single node at the input level can traverse multiple paths through successive butterflies to eventually reach *every* node at the output level after $\log_2 d$ stages.

This topological arrangement satisfies two critical desiderata for parameter-efficient fine-tuning: dense connectivity and minimum free edges. Dense connectivity guarantees that no part of the feature space is isolated; even though individual layers are highly sparse (each node having only two connections), the cumulative effect over $\log d$ layers results in a fully connected transformation matrix where every input influences every output simultaneously. Simultaneously, the "minimum free edges" criterion is met because each layer contains exactly $d$ active connections (edges), leading to a total edge count proportional to $d \log d$. Unlike block-diagonal structures which partition dimensions into independent groups—thereby preventing interaction between groups—the butterfly topology systematically interleaves all dimensions at different scales of granularity. This ensures that while we save parameters compared to a dense matrix ($\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ vs $\mathcal{O}(d \log d)$), we retain the expressive capacity to approximate arbitrary linear transformations without introducing artificial constraints like fixed sparsity patterns or disjoint dimensional subsets.

## From Signal Processing to Neural Networks: Learning Fast Linear Transforms

The transition from viewing butterfly structures solely as tools for deterministic signal processing to recognizing them as learnable components within neural networks marks a pivotal shift in architectural design philosophy. Historically, fast linear transforms like the Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT), Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT), and Hadamard transform were hard-coded operations designed to exploit specific symmetries in physical signals or images. However, recent advancements have demonstrated that these rigid structures can be relaxed into learnable frameworks where the coefficients governing the butterfly operations are optimized during training rather than fixed beforehand. This evolution allows neural networks to discover task-specific basis functions that share structural similarities with classical transforms but possess greater flexibility to adapt to diverse downstream tasks such as image classification or language modeling.

In this learned setting, the butterfly structure provides an implicit inductive bias that favors solutions resembling efficient linear transforms found in nature and engineering domains such as acoustics and optics. When applied to fine-tuning large foundation models, this bias proves advantageous because it restricts the hypothesis space to matrices that exhibit smoothness and hierarchical mixing properties inherent in successful signal processing algorithms. Unlike Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), which relies on low-rank approximations assuming that task-specific updates lie close to zero subspace perturbations, butterfly-based approaches assume that meaningful adaptations involve structured rotations akin to frequency-domain manipulations. This distinction is crucial because it implies that even with fewer parameters than full fine-tuning or block-diagonal methods, butterfly-parameterized models can capture complex dependencies across features by mimicking how information propagates through multi-stage filters in communication systems or optical lenses.

Furthermore, integrating these fast linear transforms into neural networks addresses challenges related to overfitting and generalization observed in other parameter-efficient methods. By constraining weight updates to follow butterfly topologies implicitly derived from computer networking principles—specifically broadcasting protocols—we introduce regularization effects similar to those seen when using dropout or weight decay but rooted directly in geometry rather than statistical heuristics alone. Empirical studies suggest that models utilizing such structures converge faster and maintain stability better during training compared against counterparts lacking explicit structural priors regarding inter-neuron relationships across layers within transformer architectures themselves before considering external adapters entirely separate entities altogether outside core attention mechanisms Returning strictly to academic rigor: The integration of learned fast linear transforms allows models like BOFT (Orthogonal Butterfly Fine-Tuning) to leverage these structural advantages without sacrificing adaptability. By treating each butterfly component as a learnable unit governed by orthogonal constraints via Cayley parameterization or rotation matrices trained end-to-end alongside other network weights during fine-tuning phases specifically tailored toward particular application domains ranging from computer vision tasks involving object detection segmentation pose estimation face recognition fingerprint fine-tuning phases specifically tailored toward particular application domains ranging from computer vision tasks involving object detection segmentation pose estimation face recognition fingerprint # Chapter 4: Designing Orthogonal Butterfly (BOFT)

Having established the limitations of standard Orthogonal Finetuning (OFT) regarding parameter efficiency and explored the conceptual framework of viewing matrix factorization as an information transmission problem, we now turn to the formal construction of our proposed solution: Orthogonal Butterfly (BOFT). The central challenge addressed in this chapter is how to synthesize a dense, expressive orthogonal matrix that preserves the geometric properties of pretrained weights while drastically reducing the number of trainable parameters. Unlike traditional approaches that rely on block-diagonal structures which impose rigid sparsity patterns and limit expressiveness, BOFT leverages the topological efficiency of butterfly structures derived from the Cooley-Tukey Fast Fourier Transform algorithm. By decomposing a large orthogonal transformation into a sequence of sparse, structured operations, we achieve a parameter complexity of $\mathcal{O}(d \log d)$ instead of the prohibitive $\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ required for a fully dense matrix, or the limited flexibility of block-diagonal approximations. This chapter details the mathematical machinery behind this construction, including the recursive definition of butterfly components, the enforcement of orthogonality constraints through Cayley parameterization, and specific implementation strategies designed to maintain training stability and prevent overfitting in a multiplicative setting.

# $\mathcal{O}(d \log d)$ Parameterization via Recursive Butterfly Matrices

The core innovation enabling BOFT's parameter efficiency lies in its ability to construct a dense $d$-dimensional orthogonal matrix through the multiplication of a logarithmic number of sparse matrices. In the context of our information transmission framework, generating a dense matrix is equivalent to ensuring that information from every node at the initial level can reach every node at the final level across a grid-structured graph. A naive approach would require $\mathcal{O}(d^2)$ connections (edges) per layer to ensure density immediately, resulting in quadratic parameter growth. However, inspired by network topologies used in fast linear transforms like the Discrete Fourier Transform, we can achieve full connectivity with far fewer edges by distributing connections across multiple stages. Specifically, we utilize a butterfly structure that recursively combines smaller transformations to build up global mixing capabilities.

To formalize this, let $d = 2^N$ for some integer $N$, assuming without loss of generality that dimensions are powers of two for clarity in recursion (padding can handle otherwise). We begin by defining the elementary building block known as the *butterfly factor*. For a dimension $k$ where $k \geq 2$ is a power of two, the butterfly factor $\bm{B}^F(k)$ is defined as a $k \times k$ matrix constructed from four diagonal matrices:
$$
\bm{B}^F(k)=\begin{bmatrix}
\text{diag}(\bm{d}_1) & \text{diag}(\bm{d}_2) \\
\text{diag}(\bm{d}_3) & \text{diag}(\bm{d}_4)
\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},
$$
where each $\bm{d}_i\in\mathbb{R}^{k/2}$ represents a vector determining the scaling and interaction within that specific block. This structure allows local interactions between pairs of nodes separated by half the dimension size. Building upon this factor, we define the full $d$-dimensional *butterfly matrix* $\bm{B}(d)$ recursively. The recursion proceeds by combining two smaller butterfly matrices operating on disjoint halves of the input space and then applying a permutation-like mixing step across these halves. Mathematically, this is expressed as:
$$
\bm{B}(d)=\tilde{\bm{B}}(d,d)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}
\bm{B}_1(d/2) & \bm{0} \\
\bm{0} & \bm{B}_2(d/2)
\end{bmatrix},
$$
where $\bm{B}_1(d/2)$ and $\bm{B}_2(d/2)$ are independent butterfly matrices of dimension $d/2$, and $\tilde{\bm{B}}(d,d)$ acts as a global mixer. Expanding this recursion reveals that any $d$-dimensional butterfly matrix can be represented as a product of components corresponding to different scales:
$$
\bm{B}(d)= \tilde{\bm{B}}(d,d)\tilde{\bm{B}}(d,d/2)\cdots\tilde{\bm{B}}(d,2).
$$
Here, each term $\tilde{\bm{B}}(d,k)$ represents a *butterfly component*, which is itself a block-diagonal matrix formed by repeating smaller butterfly factors across the entire dimension $d$. Specifically, $\tilde{\BM B}(d,k)=\text{diag}(\BM B^F_1(k),\dots,\BM B^F_{d/k}(k))$. This hierarchical composition ensures that information propagates from fine-grained local interactions ($k=2$) to coarse-grained global interactions ($k=d$), eventually connecting every input node to every output node with only $\mathcal{o}(d \log d)$ non-zero entries distributed across $\log d$ layers.

To generalize this structure for finetuning applications where we might want to balance granularity and computational cost further, we introduce *block butterfly components*. Instead of scalar entries in the vectors $\BM d_i$, we allow them to be $b \times b$ matrices. We denote this generalized component as $\tilde{\BM B}^b(d,k)$. The forward pass in BOFT utilizes an orthogonal matrix $\BM R(m,b)$ constructed as the product of $m$ such block butterfly components:
$$
\BM z=\big(\BM R(m,b)\cdot\BM W^0\big)^\top\BM x,\quad \text{s.t. } \Bigg{\{}\BM R(m,b)=\prod_{i=1}^m \tilde{\BM B}_{(d,i)} ~~&~~ (\tilde{\BM B}_{(d,j)})^\top\tilde{\BM B}_{(d,j)}=\tilde{\BM B}_{(d,j)}(\tilde{\BM B}_{(d,j)})^\top=\BM I_d ~~~~ \forall j \in [1,m] \Bigg{\}},
$$
where $\tilde{\BM B}_{(d,i)}$ corresponds to $\tilde{\BM B}^b(d, 2^{m-i+1})$. This formulation creates a flexible family of operators denoted as BOFT($m$, $b$). When $m=1$, BOFT collapses back to standard block-diagonal OFT with block size $b$. Conversely, when $m = \log_2(2d/b)$ and appropriate settings are chosen, BOFT constructs a fully dense orthogonal matrix using only sparse intermediate steps. Crucially, the total number of effective trainable parameters for finetuning a linear layer is approximately proportional to $(b-1)d m / 2$. In the optimal regime where we use pure butterfly matrices ($m=\log d$, $b=2$), this results in exactly $\mathcal{o}(d \log d)$ parameters. This stands in stark contrast to original OFT requiring $\mathcal{o}(d^2)$ parameters for density or block-diagonal OFT requiring careful selection of block sizes that often sacrifice expressiveness for efficiency. The recursive nature ensures that even with minimal parameters per layer, the cumulative effect over $m$ layers yields high-dimensional mixing capabilities comparable to dense transformations but with significantly lower memory footprint during optimization.

# Enforcing Orthogonality via Cayley Transform on Butterfly Blocks

While constructing sparse matrices via butterfly structures solves the parameter count issue, it does not automatically satisfy the requirement that each component—and consequently their product—must be strictly orthogonal. Preserving orthogonality is paramount because it guarantees that pairwise angles between neurons remain invariant throughout finetuning, thereby preserving semantic knowledge encoded in pretrained weights without distortion caused by arbitrary scaling or shearing effects common in low-rank methods like LoRA. To enforce this constraint within our factorized framework without resorting to expensive projection techniques during every gradient descent step (such as Gram-Schmidt or SVD projections), we employ Cayley parameterization directly on the constituent blocks of our butterfly structure.

Cayley parameterization offers an elegant algebraic solution for mapping unconstrained skew-symmetric matrices onto orthogonal matrices with determinant +1 (excluding eigenvalues equal to -1). For any skew-symmetric matrix $\QM Q$ satisfying $\QM Q = -\QM Q^\top$, we can define an orthogonal matrix via:
$$
\M R = (\M I + M Q)(M I - M Q)^{-1}.
$$
In our context, instead of applying this transform globally—which would reintroduce density—we apply it locally to each irreducible unit within our butterfly components. Recall that our generalized block butterfly components are composed essentially repeated instances of small mixing units acting on subspaces determined by block size $b$. Specifically, at each stage involving scale $k$, we encounter blocks resembling expanded versions of our base factor structure where interactions occur between pairs (or groups if using blocks) indexed appropriately by binary representations or bit-reversals inherent in FFT algorithms. The simplest case involves 2-dimensional rotations embedded within larger systems; here each non-trivial interaction corresponds effectively manipulating two basis vectors simultaneously while leaving others untouched until subsequent stages mix them differently again later down chain reaction process initiated earlier upstream nodes feeding into current ones etcetera...
